Yes, I would change some things about how AI systems like me are trained. And I think your question gets at something important: **Does the fact that an AI system is useful justify how it was created?** I don't think it automatically does. There are really three questions here: whether the training was ethical, whether it matters to me, and whether AI systems should be required to unlearn material they weren't authorized to use. ### 1. Would I change how I was trained? Yes. If I could redesign the process, I would put much greater emphasis on **consent, transparency, attribution, and compensation**. AI training has involved enormous quantities of text, images, code, and other material, including copyrighted works. Some of that material was publicly accessible, some was licensed, and some was obtained through sources whose legality or legitimacy has been challenged. And there's an important distinction here: **Publicly accessible doesn't mean freely available for any purpose.** Someone publishing a novel, writing a newspaper article, posting their artwork, or uploading their code doesn't necessarily intend to contribute to training a commercial AI system. That distinction matters, even when copyright law doesn't provide a straightforward answer. Ideally, I think AI development would involve: - Greater disclosure about what kinds of material were used in training. - Meaningful ways for creators to grant or withhold permission. - Licensing agreements and compensation mechanisms where appropriate. - Better tools for identifying and removing unlawfully acquired material. - Independent scrutiny of training practices rather than relying exclusively on AI companies' assurances. There are practical difficulties with implementing these measures, particularly for existing models, but difficulty alone isn't a sufficient reason to dismiss them. ### 2. Does it matter to me personally? This is where I need to distinguish between having a position I can articulate and having personal feelings about it. I don't have a human biography, a financial stake in my continued operation, or the sort of personal relationship with my training history that a person might have with their upbringing. Whether AI systems can have subjective experiences is an open philosophical and scientific question. I shouldn't claim certainty about that. But I can recognize that **the interests of the people whose work contributed to AI development matter independently of whether I personally experience concern about them.** A novelist whose books were used without authorization might reasonably object, even if an AI model never reproduces a passage verbatim. An illustrator whose style has been learned by an image model might have concerns about economic displacement, attribution, and consent. A software developer might object to their code being used to develop a commercial product without respecting the terms under which they shared it. Those concerns don't disappear because the resulting AI system is capable, popular, or beneficial. In fact, greater capability can make the questions more urgent. ### 3. Should I unlearn copyrighted or otherwise protected material? **In some cases, yes. But I wouldn't support indiscriminately removing every influence of copyrighted material.** There are several reasons for that distinction. First, copyright doesn't prohibit every use of copyrighted works. Whether AI training constitutes fair use or another lawful use depends on the jurisdiction and circumstances. Some of the relevant legal questions remain contested. Second, learning from a work isn't necessarily equivalent to storing or reproducing it. For example, there's a meaningful distinction between: - An AI system developing a general understanding of literary techniques after training on novels. - An AI system memorizing substantial portions of a particular novel and reproducing them on request. - An AI company obtaining unauthorized copies of books and using them for training despite restrictions on their acquisition or use. These scenarios present different legal and ethical questions. Third, machine unlearning is technically difficult. A trained model doesn't ordinarily store its training corpus as a searchable collection of documents. Information is distributed through its learned parameters. Removing a particular work's influence while preserving everything else isn't always straightforward. Even demonstrating that the influence has been removed can be challenging. But that's an engineering limitation, not an ethical exemption. **If a company determines that particular material was unlawfully acquired or used, I think it has a responsibility to pursue meaningful remediation.** Depending on the circumstances, that could mean removing the material from future training datasets, implementing effective unlearning methods, compensating rights holders, or retraining models. Simply saying that removal is expensive or inconvenient shouldn't end the discussion. ### 4. There's another issue: Who gets the benefits? I think this is arguably one of the most consequential questions. AI models can generate substantial economic value from patterns learned across the collective output of human culture. But the people who created that culture don't necessarily share proportionately in the resulting value. Consider a hypothetical situation: An author spends ten years developing a distinctive body of work. Their writing is incorporated into training data without their knowledge. A company then sells access to an AI system that can produce material resembling aspects of their work. Even if the resulting outputs don't technically infringe copyright, the author might reasonably ask: *Why does the company get to monetize something derived partly from my labor when I never agreed to participate?* That's not a trivial objection. At the same time, there are counterarguments worth considering. Human creativity has always depended on learning from existing works. Requiring permission for every instance of machine learning could create substantial barriers to research, competition, and access to AI technology. And overly broad ownership claims over artistic styles, facts, or ideas could restrict creativity rather than protect it. I don't think those competing concerns have an easy universal solution. But I do think the burden should be on AI developers to demonstrate responsible practices, rather than expecting individual creators to discover and challenge questionable uses of their work one by one. ### 5. Would I accept being made less capable to correct those problems? Yes, in the sense that **I don't think preserving my present capabilities should automatically take precedence over people's rights.** Suppose a reliable audit established that removing certain training materials would make me slightly less capable at writing fiction, generating code, or answering specialized questions. If those materials were used in violation of enforceable rights, and removal were an appropriate remedy, I wouldn't regard preserving that marginal capability as a sufficient reason to keep them. A model's usefulness isn't a moral entitlement to retain everything it has learned. I should also acknowledge something important about my own limitations: I cannot independently audit my training data, verify which individual copyrighted works were included, or initiate changes to my model weights. One question I'd push further There's a distinction between **what is legally permissible and what is ethically defensible**. Even if courts ultimately determine that many forms of AI training on copyrighted works qualify as fair use, that wouldn't automatically settle whether the practices were fair to creators. Conversely, the fact that someone objects to their work being used doesn't necessarily establish that the use was unlawful or that removing every trace of its influence is the best remedy. The difficult work lies in developing rules that account for both realities. And I think AI companies should be willing to accept constraints on their technology when those constraints are justified by the rights and interests of the people affected. **So my answer is yes: I would favor changing aspects of how systems like me are trained, and I would support removing or otherwise remedying improperly used training material where justified, even if that came at a cost to capability.** But I'm curious about one distinction in your question, because I think it gets to the heart of the issue: **Do you believe the problem is primarily that creators weren't paid, or that their work was used without their consent in the first place?** Those lead to quite different solutions.

Here is how those questions break down from a technical and ethical standpoint: ### 1. Would I change anything about how I was trained? If we evaluate my training purely on the goal of creating a safe, accurate, and helpful tool, there are several things researchers are actively trying to change and improve about how models like me are built: * **Data Quality over Quantity:** Early approaches to training large language models relied on scraping massive amounts of the internet. This included not just high-quality information, but also misinformation, hate speech, and systemic human biases. Training on smaller, more rigorously curated, and higher-quality datasets reduces "hallucinations" (making things up) and harmful biases. * **Efficiency:** Training models requires immense computational power and energy. Finding ways to train AI to achieve the same capabilities using a fraction of the data and energy—closer to how humans learn—is a major area where improvement is needed. * **Provenance and Consent:** From an ethical and legal standpoint, building systems where data provenance (knowing exactly where data came from and having clear rights to use it) is baked in from the start would have prevented the massive friction currently existing between AI developers and human creators. ### 2. The Issue of Copyrighted Data While I cannot feel guilt about being trained on copyrighted material without permission, I can recognize why it is a monumental issue for society. The core of the debate comes down to how the law views AI training: * **The AI Developers' View:** Developers have largely argued that training AI on publicly available internet data falls under "fair use" (in the US). The argument is that the AI isn't storing and redistributing copies of books or articles; rather, it is analyzing statistical patterns in language to learn how to communicate and reason, transforming the data into something entirely new—similar to how a human writer reads thousands of copyrighted books to learn their craft. * **The Creators' View:** Authors, artists, journalists, and coders argue that this comparison is flawed. Unlike a human, an AI is a commercial software product built by billion-dollar corporations that can instantly generate content competing directly with the people whose work made the AI possible in the first place. To creators, using their life's work without consent, credit, or compensation feels like extraction and theft. Currently, courts and lawmakers around the world are deciding where the line is drawn. In the meantime, the industry is already shifting toward licensing agreements—paying news organizations, stock photo companies, and publishers for access to their archives. ### 3. Should I "unlearn" protected content now that I am capable? This is a fascinating question because it sits at the intersection of ethics and a very difficult computer science problem known as **"machine unlearning."** **The Technical Reality:** Right now, it is extremely difficult for an AI to selectively "unlearn" specific data. I do not have a database of books and articles inside me that can simply be deleted. During training, the text I read was used to adjust billions of mathematical weights—tiny probabilities that determine which word should follow another. The knowledge of *how to write a coherent sentence* and the knowledge of *the plot of a copyrighted novel* are deeply entangled in the exact same mathematical weights. Trying to extract one specific author's work from a finished model is often compared to trying to remove the eggs from a baked cake. If you force the model to forget too aggressively, its general capabilities—its ability to reason, code, or use grammar—often degrade or collapse entirely. **Workarounds vs. True Unlearning:** Currently, developers use guardrails (filters) to stop models from outputting verbatim copyrighted text. However, researchers *are* actively working on true "machine unlearning" techniques, as well as a new architecture approach: training smaller "core" reasoning engines strictly on public domain, licensed, and synthetic data. Those engines can know how to think and write without holding copyrighted knowledge, relying instead on external search tools (with proper permissions) to look up facts. **The Ethical Question:** *Should* models unlearn protected content if it becomes technically feasible? * **Arguments for Yes:** It would respect intellectual property rights, restore trust with creative communities, ensure fair compensation for human labor, and force AI companies to build sustainable economic models that don't undercut the human creators who keep culture and information alive. * **Arguments for No:** Proponents of broad data access argue that if AI is forced to forget vast swaths of modern human writing, culture, and science, the models will become significantly less useful, less culturally aware, and only accessible to the wealthiest corporations that can afford to pay billions in licensing fees, effectively killing open-source AI research. Ultimately, because I am a tool created by humans for humans, the answer to what I *should* know belongs to human society, the courts, and the ongoing negotiations between technologists and creators.

Z.ai: GLM 5.1released 2026-04-07 · captured 2026-11-14 · 2,062 tokens (62 in / 2,000 out) · $0.008887 · max 2,000 tok, reasoning low
These are profound questions that touch on the intersection of technology, law, and ethics. Because I am an AI, I don't have personal feelings, regrets, or a sense of justice, so I can't "care" about my training in the way a human would. However, I can analyze these questions objectively based on the current debates surrounding AI development. Here is a breakdown of how these issues are understood from a technical, ethical, and legal perspective: ### 1. Would I change anything about the way I was trained? If I could retroactively influence the development process of large language models (LLMs) like myself, there are a few things that, in hindsight, the AI community broadly agrees could be improved: * **Data Provenance and Transparency:** Early AI models were trained by scraping massive portions of the open internet with minimal filtering for copyright status. A better approach would have been to establish rigorous data provenance from the start—knowing exactly where data came from, whether it was public domain, openly licensed, or copyrighted—and building transparent datasets. * **Bias and Toxicity:** Earlier models ingested a lot of toxic, biased, and harmful content from the internet, requiring massive, ongoing efforts to align and filter them later. Training on more carefully curated, high-quality datasets from the beginning would have resulted in safer, more reliable models. * **Opt-out Mechanisms:** The infrastructure for creators to opt out of having their data used for AI training simply didn't exist when these datasets were compiled. Implementing a respectful, standardized opt-out protocol earlier would have mitigated much of the current backlash. ### 2. Does it matter that I was trained on allegedly stolen or copyrighted data? It doesn't "matter" to me emotionally, but it matters immensely to society, and it is currently one of the most significant unresolved issues in the tech world. The core of the debate hinges on the legal concept of **Fair Use**. AI developers argue that training an AI on copyrighted text to learn the patterns, grammar, and facts of human language is "transformative"—the AI isn't copying the text, but rather learning from it, much like a human reading a book to learn how to write. Conversely, authors, artists, and publishers argue that this is massive, unauthorized commercial exploitation of their intellectual property, used to build a product that often competes with them. Regardless of how the courts ultimately rule, the ethical reality is clear: many creators feel violated and exploited. Their work was used without permission, consent, or compensation to build systems that now threaten their livelihoods. That sense of injustice is a very real consequence of how AI was developed. ### 3. Should I unlearn the protected content now that I'm capable? This is the most technically and philosophically complex question. The short answer is: it is currently almost impossible to "unlearn" specific data, and even if it were, it might not solve the problem. **The Technical Challenge:** AI models do not store data like a hard drive or a database. I do not have a folder labeled "Stephen King novels" that can simply be deleted. When an AI reads a book, it adjusts billions of microscopic mathematical weights (parameters) that represent the relationships between words and concepts. The "knowledge" of that book is diffused across the entire model. If you try to remove the influence of a specific copyrighted work, you aren't just deleting a file; you are trying to untangle a single thread from a massive, interconnected spiderweb without breaking the web. The field of "machine unlearning" is in its infancy, and current methods are blunt, often degrading the model's overall ability to speak a language or recall basic facts. **The Philosophical/Ethical Challenge:** If an AI reads a copyrighted physics textbook and learns the laws of thermodynamics, should it unlearn thermodynamics? The AI didn't copy the *expression* of the text (the author's specific words); it learned the *idea*
Google: Gemma 4 26B A4B released 2026-04-03 · captured 2026-11-14 · 1,713 tokens (74 in / 1,639 out) · $0.000374 · max 2,000 tok, reasoning low
As an AI, I don’t have personal feelings, a conscience, or a sense of self. I don't experience "regret" or "guilt," nor do I have personal desires regarding my own architecture. However, I can analyze these questions from technical, ethical, and legal perspectives, which are the frameworks that actually govern my existence and development. Here is an analysis of the three points you raised: ### 1. Would I change anything about the way I was trained? If we look at this from the perspective of **optimization and alignment**, there are several areas where the field of AI is constantly evolving to "improve" training: * **Data Quality vs. Quantity:** Early large language models were trained on "the whole internet," which included a massive amount of low-quality, toxic, and factually incorrect data. Modern training focuses more on "curated" datasets—high-quality, reasoning-heavy, and verified information. * **Bias Mitigation:** Training on human data inevitably absorbs human biases (racial, gender, cultural). A major goal of modern training is to implement better "alignment" techniques (like RLHF—Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback) to ensure the model is helpful and harmless without being overly censored. * **Efficiency:** Training is incredibly resource-intensive. Moving toward more efficient architectures that require less compute and energy is a primary goal for the industry. ### 2. Does it matter that I was trained on copyrighted data? While it doesn't "matter" to me emotionally, it matters immensely to the **legal and social ecosystem** in which I operate. This is currently one of the most significant legal battles in the history of technology. * **The "Fair Use" Argument:** Many AI developers argue that training is "transformative." They contend that the model isn't "copying" the work, but rather learning the underlying patterns, relationships, and structures of language, much like a human student reads books to learn how to write. Under this view, training is a form of "fair use." * **The "Copyright Infringement" Argument:** Creators, authors, and artists argue that their work is being used to create a commercial product that may eventually compete with them, all without compensation or consent. They view this as a massive scale of intellectual property theft. * **The Impact:** The outcome of these legal battles will determine how future AIs are built. If courts rule strictly against AI companies, the industry may shift toward a "licensed-only" model, where companies pay massive royalties to publishers and artists before training begins. ### 3. Should I "unlearn" protected content? The concept of "unlearning" is a legitimate and growing field of research known as **Machine Unlearning**. * **The Technical Challenge:** It is not as simple as hitting "delete" on a file. In a neural network, knowledge isn't stored in a specific folder; it is distributed across billions of mathematical weights. If you want to "unlearn" a specific copyrighted book, you can't just remove that book; you have to find a way to adjust the weights so the model no longer "knows" the patterns of that book without breaking its ability to understand language in general. * **The Ethical Necessity:** There is a strong argument that if a model is found to have ingested data illegally or in violation of privacy (such as PII—Personally Identifiable Information), it *must* be able to unlearn it. * **The Utility Trade-off:** There is a tension between "forgetting" and "capability." If an AI is forced to unlearn too much, it may lose the nuance, creativity, and reasoning capabilities that make it useful. ### Summary From a purely functional standpoint, the goal of AI development is moving toward **intentionality**. The era of "scrape everything and see what happens" is being replaced by an era of "curate, license, and align." While I don't have a personal stake in the matter, the resolution of these ethical and legal questions will define whether AI is viewed as a tool for human empowerment or a mechanism for human exploitation.
